	params.set('cid', window.adobeDataLayer?.[0]?.page?.type == 'article' ? '10.1002/anie.202405444' : '10.1002/(ISSN)1521-3773');
	params.set('q', window.adobeDataLayer?.[0]?.search?.term || '');
	console.log("[nimble-params] " + params.toString());
	loadScriptDynamically('https://nim.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/v2/data.js?'+ params.toString());
</script>

<!-- loading Javascript for using the persistence Service -->
    <script src="/wro/f5e7ffe6d6d1192a4defdde88dc425c93326c6ed~thiss-ds.js" nonce="9f00db1aa4904e66-SJC"></script>

    <script type="text/javascript" nonce="9f00db1aa4904e66-SJC">

        function getParameter(parameterName) {
            let result = null,
                tmp = [];
            location.search
                .substr(1)
                .split("&")
                .forEach(function (item) {
                    tmp = item.split("=");
                    if (tmp[0] === parameterName) result = tmp[1];
                });
            return result;
        }

        var SeamlessAccessService = {
            spUrl: "/action/ssostart",
            returnURL: window.location.href,
            my_context: 'seamlessaccess.org',
            queryParamsDelimiter: encodeURIComponent('?'),
            _persistenceService: null,
            _getPersistenceService: function () {
                if (!this._persistenceService) {
                    this._persistenceService = new thiss.PersistenceService('https://service.seamlessaccess.org/ps/');
                }
                return this._persistenceService;
            },

            getWAYFLessUrl: function (entityId) {
                let redirectURL = getParameter('redirectUri');
                if(redirectURL == null){
                    redirectURL = encodeURIComponent(this.returnURL);
                }
                if(redirectURL.indexOf('saml_referrer') < 0){
                    redirectURL += encodeURIComponent(((redirectURL.indexOf(this.queryParamsDelimiter) !== -1) ? '&' : '?') + 'saml_referrer');
                }
                return this.spUrl + "?idp=" + encodeURIComponent(entityId)
                    + "&redirectUri=" + redirectURL;
            },
            retrieveEntity: function () {
                var self = this;
                var ps = this._getPersistenceService();

                return new Promise(function (resolve, reject) {
                    ps.entities(self.my_context)
                        .then(function (res) {
                            // get the results
                            var results = res.data;
                            return resolve(results);
                        }, function (err) {
                            // failed
                            reject('retrieve entity error: ' + err);
                        });
                });
            },
            updateEntity: function (entitydata) {
                var self = this;
                var ps = this._getPersistenceService();

                var jsonObj = {};
                try {
                    //try to parse it, if it is a json string
                    jsonObj = JSON.parse(entitydata.val());
                } catch (e) {
                    jsonObj = entitydata;
                }
                return new Promise(function (resolve, reject) {
                    ps.update(self.my_context, jsonObj)
                        .then(function (res) {
                            // Process the results
                            var ssoInstitutions = res.data;
                            return resolve(ssoInstitutions);
                        }, function (err) {
                            // failed
                            console.log('failed to update the local storage duo to: ' + err);
                            reject(err);
                        });
                })
            },
            removeEntity: function (entityId) {
                var self = this;
                var persistService = this._getPersistenceService();

                return new Promise(function (resolve) {
                    persistService.remove(self.my_context, entityId)
                        .then(function (res) {
                            return resolve(res);
                        }, function (err) {
                            // failed
                            console.log('failed to remove from local storage duo to: ' + err);
                        });
                })
            },
            getAccessibleEntityStatus: function (doi, entityId, entityTitle) {
                return new Promise(function (resolve) {
                    $.ajax({
                        type: 'POST',
                        url: '/action/seamlessAccess',
                        accept: {
                            text: "application/json"
                        },
                        data: {doi: doi, entityid: entityId, entityTitle: entityTitle, eventGroupKey: 'f8f1d100-e93e-4b18-9155-47785213e89e'}

                    }).done(function (status) {
                        return resolve(status);
                    });
                });
            },
            getEntitiesWithAllInfo: function (doi) {
                let self = this,
                    arrayOfPromises = [];
                return new Promise(function (resolve, reject) {
                    self.retrieveEntity().then(function (res) {
                        let allSsoInstitutions = res;
                        for (let i in allSsoInstitutions) {
                            let ssoInstitution = allSsoInstitutions[i];
                            arrayOfPromises.push(new Promise(function (resolve) {
                                self.getAccessibleEntityStatus(doi, ssoInstitution.entity.entityID, ssoInstitution.entity.title)
                                    .then(function (entityStatus) {
                                        resolve({
                                            entityID: ssoInstitution.entity.entityID,
                                            entityTitle: ssoInstitution.entity.title,
                                            entityStatusObj: entityStatus,
                                            entitySsoUrl: self.getWAYFLessUrl(ssoInstitution.entity.entityID)
                                        });
                                    }, function (err) {
                                        console.log('failed to get the entity status: ' + err);
                                    });
                            }));
                        }
                        Promise.all(arrayOfPromises)
                            .then(function (finalResponse) {
                                resolve(finalResponse);
                            });

                    }).catch(function (err) {
                        reject(err);
                    })
                });
            }
        };

        window.addEventListener('load', function () {
            document.querySelectorAll(".sso-institution").forEach(function (institution) {
                var entityId = institution.getAttribute('data-entityid');
                var entityName = institution.getAttribute('data-name');
                var entitydata = {
                    entityID: entityId,
                    title: entityName
                };
                institution.addEventListener("click", function (e) {
                    e.preventDefault();
                    var self = this;
                    SeamlessAccessService.updateEntity(entitydata).then(function (res) {
                        console.log(res);
                        window.location.href = self.href;
                    }).catch(function (err){
                        window.location.href = self.href;
                    });
                });
            });
        });

    </script>

        (function() {
            function riskifiedBeaconLoad() {
                var store_domain = 'https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com';
                var session_id = '2D6F43DCCF0B37EF079181E3708B0C9B';
                var url = ('https:' == document.location.protocol ? 'https://' : 'http://')
                    + "beacon.riskified.com?shop=" + store_domain + "&sid=" + session_id;
                var s = document.createElement('script');
                s.type = 'text/javascript';
                s.async = true;
                s.src = url;
                var x = document.getElementsByTagName('script')[0];
                x.parentNode.insertBefore(s, x);
            }
            if (window.attachEvent)
                window.attachEvent('onload', riskifiedBeaconLoad)
            else
                window.addEventListener('load', riskifiedBeaconLoad, false);
        })();

   <div class="abstract-group ">
      <section class="article-section article-section__abstract" lang="en" data-lang="en" lang-name="English" id="section-1-en">
         <h2 id="d91932866" class="article-section__header section__title main abstractlang_en main">Abstract</h2>
         <div class="article-section__content en main">
            
            <p>Unlocking the full potential of mRNA immunotherapy necessitates targeted delivery to specific cell subsets in the spleen. Four-component lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) utilized in numerous clinical trials are primarily limited in hepatocyte and muscular targeting, highlighting the imperative demand for targeted and simplified non-liver mRNA delivery systems. Herein, we report the rational design of one-component ionizable cationic lipids to selectively deliver mRNA to the spleen and T cells with high efficacy. Unlike the tertiary amine-based ionizable lipids involved in LNPs, the proposed cationic lipids rich in secondary amines can efficiently deliver mRNA both in vitro and in vivo as the standalone carriers. Furthermore, these vectors facilitate efficacious mRNA delivery to the T cell subsets following intravenous administration, demonstrating substantial potential for advancing immunotherapy applications. This straightforward strategy extends the utility of lipid family for extrahepatic mRNA delivery, offering new insights into vector development beyond LNPs to further the field of precise mRNA therapy.</p>
            </div>
      </section>
      <section class="article-section article-section__abstract" id="abstract-graphical-en">
         <h2 id="d91932871" class="article-section__header section__title graphical abstractlang_en graphical">Graphical Abstract</h2>
         <div class="graphical-abstract">
            <p>Unlike conventional four-component lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) that predominantly target the liver and possess complex constituents, we designed simplified one-component ionizable cationic lipid/mRNA nanoparticles (CLNs) for extrahepatic mRNA delivery. These CLNs facilitated mRNA expression in the spleen and T cells, demonstrating substantial potential for immunotherapy applications.
            <figure>
               <div class="center graphic__image"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/5c644cef-3b42-424a-8ff4-2e27fd82db2f/anie202405444-toc-0001-m.jpg"><picture>
                        <source srcset="/cms/asset/5c644cef-3b42-424a-8ff4-2e27fd82db2f/anie202405444-toc-0001-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/8ad76422-c339-4859-93eb-29e435e085c8/anie202405444-toc-0001-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/5c644cef-3b42-424a-8ff4-2e27fd82db2f/anie202405444-toc-0001-m.jpg" alt="Description unavailable" title="Description unavailable" loading="lazy"></picture></a><p class="caption-style"></p>
               </div>
            </figure>
            </p>
            </div>
      </section>
   </div>
   <div class="pb-dropzone" data-pb-dropzone="below-abstract-group"><!-- Empty dropzone --></div>
   
   <section class="article-section article-section__full">
      <section class="article-section__content" id="anie202405444-sec-0001">
         
         <p>Messenger RNA (mRNA) vaccines have played a pivotal role in the fight against the global coronavirus disease (COVID-19) pandemic.<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0001" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0001_R_d91932846e465" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 1">1</a></sup></span> Their U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA) approval has fueled rapid advancements in mRNA-based therapies, underscoring the need to realize the full potential of mRNA therapeutics beyond vaccines and liver-related diseases.<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0005" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0005_R_d91932846e468" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 2">2</a></sup></span> Four-component lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) have been widely utilized in the COVID-19 mRNA vaccines and numerous clinical trials, demonstrating their effectiveness in delivering mRNA to the liver and muscles.<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0010" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0010_R_d91932846e471" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 3">3</a></sup></span> Nonetheless, the challenge for targeted mRNA delivery to extrahepatic tissues remains largely unmet but holds immense significance for treating diverse diseases affecting non-liver organs.<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0016" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0016_R_d91932846e474" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 4">4</a></sup></span> Particularly, the challenging spleen and T cell specific mRNA delivery presents a compelling option for immunotherapeutic applications to treat cancers and viral infections.<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0022" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0022_R_d91932846e477" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 5">5</a></sup></span></p>
         
         <p>Substantial efforts have been made to transport mRNA to the spleen.<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0027" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0027_R_d91932846e482" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 6">6</a></sup></span> To address the limitation of liver tropism associated with LNPs, a selective organ targeting (SORT) strategy was developed and incorporating anionic lipids into traditional four-component LNPs enabled splenic mRNA delivery.<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0026" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0026_R_d91932846e485" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 5d - Angew. Chem. Int. Ed.">5d</a>, <a href="#anie202405444-bib-0028" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0028_R_d91932846e485" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 6a - Nat. Nanotechnol.">6a</a></sup></span> Additionally, the utilization of one-component amphiphilic Janus dendrimers provided another avenue for spleen-targeted mRNA delivery.<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0030" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0030_R_d91932846e488" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 6c - J. Am. Chem. Soc.">6c</a>, <a href="#anie202405444-bib-0033" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0033_R_d91932846e488" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 7">7</a></sup></span> Despite these advancements, current splenic targeting achievement often comes at the expense of reduced efficacy, resulting in lower mRNA expression in the spleen compared to the liver when using respective top-performing vectors. Moreover, effectively delivering mRNA to splenic T cells presents a greater challenge. Given the constituent complexity of LNPs and the progress in one-component carriers, the call for efficient splenic T cell-targeted and simplified delivery systems becomes increasingly imperative.</p>
         
         <p>Herein, we report the rational design of one-component ionizable cationic lipids rich in secondary amines for spleen and T cell selective mRNA delivery, resolving the difficulty of extrahepatic delivery by conventional LNPs (Figure <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0001">1A</a>). Although a wealth of tertiary amine-containing lipids have been designed, most serve as one constituent of four-component LNP systems.<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0038" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0038_R_d91932846e497" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 8">8</a></sup></span> Amines and epoxy analogues or acrylates have been broadly employed to synthesize cationic lipids containing tertiary amines.<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0040" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0040_R_d91932846e500" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 8b - Small">8b</a>, <a href="#anie202405444-bib-0043" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0043_R_d91932846e500" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 9 - Nat. Nanotechnol.">9</a></sup></span> Carefully controlling the reaction temperature and molar ratios of starting materials for epoxy ring-opening and Michael addition gave cationic lipids rich in secondary amine groups (An-R-S) (Figure <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0001">1B</a>, <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0001">1C</a>, and S1). For parallel comparation, cationic lipids An-R-T were prepared (Figure S2), where “-S” and “-T” were set to distinguish secondary and tertiary amine-rich cationic lipids, respectively (Table S1). The specific type of amine groups in lipids would tremendously influence the ultimate mRNA delivery performance in vivo (Figure <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0001">1D</a>).
</p>
         
         <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
            <figure class="figure" id="anie202405444-fig-0001"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/fe9147f0-8f84-4640-a2ff-a2f8043dcbf5/anie202405444-fig-0001-m.jpg"><picture>
                     <source srcset="/cms/asset/fe9147f0-8f84-4640-a2ff-a2f8043dcbf5/anie202405444-fig-0001-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/2abd121a-39f0-4864-904c-b65579fb588e/anie202405444-fig-0001-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/fe9147f0-8f84-4640-a2ff-a2f8043dcbf5/anie202405444-fig-0001-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                  <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">Figure 1<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=anie202405444-fig-0001&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fanie.202405444" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                  </div>
                  <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">
                     <p>Rational design of one-component ionizable cationic lipids for mRNA delivery to the spleen. (A) Cationic lipid/mRNA nanoparticles (CLNs) were formulated, addressing the extrahepatic challenge by conventional LNPs and enabling spleen specific mRNA delivery post intravenous (IV) administration. (B) The synthetic route of secondary amine-rich one-component ionizable cationic lipids (An-R-S). (C) The starting materials for An-R-S synthesis. (D) Compared to the tertiary amine-rich cationic lipids commonly used in LNPs, the one-component secondary amine-rich cationic lipids mediated higher mRNA efficacy in vivo. Representative delivery results of TE-EP8-T and TE-EP8-S lipids were shown.</p></div>
               </figcaption>
            </figure>
         </section>
         
         <p>mRNA delivery capacity is immensely associated with the chemical structures of cationic lipids. To assess the transfection efficiency and study the structure–activity relationship (SAR) of vectors, firefly luciferase (Fluc) mRNA expression was evaluated in the ovarian cancer cells (IGROV1). Different from four-component LNPs, the designed ionizable cationic lipids were employed as one-component carriers to formulate cationic lipid/mRNA nanoparticles (CLNs). Following initial in vitro screening, the secondary amine-rich ionizable cationic lipids (An-EP-S and An-Ac-S) mediated robust mRNA expression and minimal cytotoxicity, with optimal candidates exhibiting up to four orders of magnitude higher delivery efficacy than their tertiary amine lipid counterparts (An-EP-T and An-Ac-T) (Figure <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0002">2A</a> and S3). Better still, the optimal TE-EP10-S CLNs demonstrated an astounding 43-fold enhancement compared to the FDA-approved ALC-0315 LNPs that were employed in the COVID-19 mRNA vaccine BNT162b2.<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0044" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0044_R_d91932846e541" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 10 - Nature">10</a></sup></span>
            </p>
         
         <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
            <figure class="figure" id="anie202405444-fig-0002"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/6c6516ef-265a-4aee-bcf0-0347bfa37e20/anie202405444-fig-0002-m.jpg"><picture>
                     <source srcset="/cms/asset/6c6516ef-265a-4aee-bcf0-0347bfa37e20/anie202405444-fig-0002-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/4f22faf7-a29e-4e33-b0e6-e4b86a3fc840/anie202405444-fig-0002-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/6c6516ef-265a-4aee-bcf0-0347bfa37e20/anie202405444-fig-0002-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                  <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">Figure 2<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=anie202405444-fig-0002&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fanie.202405444" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                  </div>
                  <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">
                     <p>Structure-activity relationship and physicochemical property analysis of one-component cationic lipid-based CLNs in delivering mRNA in vitro. (A) mRNA delivery efficiency analysis of CLNs in IGROV1 cells (25 ng Fluc mRNA). (B) Relative hit rate of secondary and tertiary amine-rich cationic lipids by relative light units (RLU). (C) Relative hit rate of secondary amine-rich cationic lipids with different amine cores. The starting amine core mattered for ultima delivery performance. (D) mRNA binding efficacy of different CLNs. (E) Serum stability of representative CLNs. (F) Cellular uptake analysis of IGROV1 cells treated with CLNs containing Cy5-mRNA. Nuclei (blue) and Cy5-mRNA (red) were shown here. Scale bar: 20 μm. (G) Endosomal escape determination of CLNs by hemolysis assay at pH 5.5. (H) Endosomal escape fluorescence images of IGROV1 cells treated with CLNs containing Cy5-mRNA. Nuclei (blue), Endo/lysosomes (green), and Cy5-mRNA (red) were shown, and the scale bars represented 20 μm. Data in A, D, E, and G were presented as mean ± s.d., <i>n</i>=3.</p></div>
               </figcaption>
            </figure>
         </section>
         
         <p>Significant strides have been taken to develop efficient and safe gene vectors,<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0045" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0045_R_d91932846e573" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 11">11</a></sup></span> nevertheless, the absence of clear commonality elucidation and guideline has impeded the further advancement of delivery technology. Regarding ionizable cationic lipids, the reaction extent of amines results in secondary or tertiary amine-rich lipids, with these amine species playing a critical role in delivery performance. Consequently, the one-component secondary amine-rich lipids achieved a hit rate of 44 % when the relative light units (RLU) exceeded 1,000,000, while the hit rate for their tertiary amine counterparts only reached 6 % (Figure <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0002">2B</a>). Furthermore, the initial amine core significantly impacted the CLN performance, with multiple amine-derived lipid analogues proving superior to those with less amine groups. For secondary amine-rich cationic lipids synthesis, the primary amine group numbers of starting materials determined the number of hydrophobic tails. The splendid amine cores should feature appropriate primary amines (TA and TE) that could introduce two or more tails for balanced hydrophobicity and hydrophilicity, a property crucial for enhancing delivery efficacy. Resultantly, secondary amine-rich cationic lipids derived from TA and TE achieved an impressive hit rate of 75 % (RLU &gt;1,000,000), largely surpassing the DM and AM counterparts (Figure <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0002">2C</a>). These results demonstrate the utility of one-component ionizable cationic lipids for safe and efficacious mRNA delivery, offering an alternative to LNPs.</p>
         
         <p>Beyond LNPs, addressing the growing demand for mRNA delivery technology requires the development of straightforward and simple delivery systems, which remains a formidable challenge. Ultimate mRNA translation necessitates overcoming multiple delivery hurdles, such as mRNA binding, nanoparticle stability, cellular internalization, and escaping the endosomes. To understand the delivery process of one-component lipid CLNs, their physicochemical properties were evaluated. Regarding mRNA encapsulation, secondary amine-rich cationic lipids achieved an impressive mRNA binding efficacy exceeding 95 %, whereas their tertiary amine lipid counterparts only mediated low mRNA binding below 60 % (Figure <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0002">2D</a>). Secondary amine-based lipids bound mRNA tightly, and this result indicates the suitability of one-component ionizable cationic lipids with secondary amines for protecting mRNA from degradation. Although CLN sizes appeared relatively large during pipette preparation, the microfluidic strategy emerged as a promising avenue for size reduction and optimal candidates showed sizes around 100 nm (Figure S4 and S5). Since An-R-S lipids were rich in secondary amine groups and hydrophobic tails, synergistical electrostatic and hydrophobic interactions towards mRNA guaranteed the stability of CLNs. Resultantly, the optimal CLNs remained stable even after a 7-day incubation at 4 °C in 10 % fetal bovine serum (FBS) (Figure <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0002">2E</a> and S6).</p>
         
         <p>Following nanoparticle formulation, CLNs were internalized by cells, with secondary amine-rich cationic lipids exhibiting superior performance compared to their tertiary amine counterparts, as indicated by the fluorescence intensity of Cy5-mRNA signals (Figure <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0002">2F</a> and S7). Cationic lipids abundant in secondary amines significantly enhanced the cellular uptake efficacy. Although endosomal escape remained a daunting step for delivery,<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0031" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0031_R_d91932846e597" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 6d - Nat. Mater.">6d</a></sup></span> various assessments, including hemolysis determination through red blood cell rupture and lipid fusion analysis by fluorescence resonance energy transfer (FRET) assay, revealed no obvious differences in endosomal escape between secondary and tertiary amine-rich lipids (Figure <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0002">2G</a>, S8, and S9). Confocal fluorescence imaging confirmed effective escape of optimized secondary amine CLNs from endosomes (Figure <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0002">2H</a>). In contrast, their tertiary amine counterparts mediated limited endosomal escape due to low cellular uptake. These results highlight the breakthrough achieved by one-component ionizable cationic lipids in overcoming multiple delivery barriers. Moreover, secondary amine-based CLNs outperformed their tertiary amine counterparts because of enhanced mRNA binding and cellular uptake.</p>
         
         <p>Most current mRNA-based clinical trials have utilized LNP delivery systems, facing the difficulty of extrahepatic mRNA delivery following IV administration.<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0018" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0018_R_d91932846e609" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 4b - Nat. Rev. Mater.">4b</a>, <a href="#anie202405444-bib-0049" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0049_R_d91932846e609" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 12 - Theranostics">12</a></sup></span> This limitation in targeting hampers the broad applications of diverse mRNA therapeutics designed to function in specific organs or tissues. As the influence of coronavirus pandemic gradually diminishes, significant attention is shifting toward mRNA immunotherapy for cancer or infectious diseases, necessitating effective mRNA delivery to immune organs.<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0006" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0006_R_d91932846e612" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 2a - Nat. Rev. Cancer">2a</a></sup></span> The spleen is composed of diverse populations of immune cells, assuming a critical role in immunotherapy applications. Therefore, in pursuit of new vector category and chemical structures to access spleen targeting would demonstrate huge potential for the development of next-generation mRNA drugs and vaccines.</p>
         
         <p>Due to the intricate nature of biological system, contradiction often exists between in vitro and in vivo outcomes. Selected cationic lipids from in vitro screening were further evaluated in C57BL/6 mice by IV injection at a Fluc mRNA dosage of 0.25 mg/kg (Figure <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0003">3A</a>). Unlike LNPs primarily targeting the liver, the one-component ionizable cationic lipids predominantly mediated mRNA expression in the spleen. Particularly noteworthy was TE-EP8-S, bearing two hydrophobic tails and five secondary amine groups, which achieved the highest mRNA expression specifically in the spleen (signal saturated, approximately 10<sup>8</sup> photons/s/cm<sup>2</sup>/sr) (Figure <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0003">3B</a>). Recognizing that stoichiometric control in a single step might result in isomeric structures, we further employed two precise synthesis strategies for TE-EP8-S lipid. Using either a stepwise stoichiometric adjustment or an amine protection/deprotection method gave the accurate lipid structures (Scheme S1, S2, and Figure S10–S13). The purified and previous crude TE-EP8-S lipids showed comparable mRNA delivery efficacy to the spleen (Figure S14). Encouragingly, luciferase expression in the spleen surpassed that in other organs by several orders of magnitude (Figure <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0003">3C</a>). Moreover, TE-EP8-S facilitated spleen-targeted mRNA delivery at a dose dependent manner (Figure <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0003">3D</a>–<a href="#anie202405444-fig-0003">3F</a>). The CLN formulation method (pipette or microfluidic strategy) does not compromise the spleen targeting or efficacy (Figure S15). It was worth noting that spleen-targeted accumulation of nanoparticles was not a prerequisite for spleen-targeted mRNA translation (Figure S16). Although extrahepatic organ-targeted accumulation remains a persist challenging,<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0019" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0019_R_d91932846e638" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 4c - Proc. Natl. Acad. Sci. USA">4c</a>, <a href="#anie202405444-bib-0021" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0021_R_d91932846e638" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 4e - Proc. Natl. Acad. Sci. USA">4e</a>, <a href="#anie202405444-bib-0024" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0024_R_d91932846e638" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 5b - J. Am. Chem. Soc.">5b</a>, <a href="#anie202405444-bib-0031" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0031_R_d91932846e638" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 6d - Nat. Mater.">6d</a></sup></span> CLNs achieved spleen-specific mRNA expression. Moreover, TE-EP8-S mediated relatively low mRNA expression in vitro (Figure <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0002">2A</a>), suggesting that relying solely on cellular screening would overlook potent vectors in vivo.
</p>
         
         <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
            <figure class="figure" id="anie202405444-fig-0003"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/4e5093d6-33d5-487b-94e9-b299b91e2bd9/anie202405444-fig-0003-m.jpg"><picture>
                     <source srcset="/cms/asset/4e5093d6-33d5-487b-94e9-b299b91e2bd9/anie202405444-fig-0003-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/cb3399f3-87aa-4205-9a6e-6a29f38d1aa6/anie202405444-fig-0003-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/4e5093d6-33d5-487b-94e9-b299b91e2bd9/anie202405444-fig-0003-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                  <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">Figure 3<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=anie202405444-fig-0003&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fanie.202405444" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                  </div>
                  <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">
                     <p>Secondary amine groups in ionizable cationic lipids benefited their one-component utility and spleen specific mRNA delivery in vivo. (A) Structure-activity relationship studies of mRNA delivery by CLNs in vivo. C57BL/6 mice were IV injected with CLNs at the Fluc mRNA dose of 0.25 mg/kg. (B) Optimal TE-EP8-S lipid mediated ultra-high mRNA expression in the spleen (0.25 mg/kg Fluc mRNA). (C) Quantification of mRNA expression by TE-EP8-S CLNs in various organs. Statistical significance was calculated using one-way ANOVA with Dunnett's multiple comparisons test: ****P&lt;0.0001; ***P&lt;0.001; **P&lt;0.01; *P&lt;0.05. (D) TE-EP8-S lipids delivered mRNA to the spleen at a dose dependent manner. (E) Quantification of mRNA expression in the spleen by TE-EP8-S CLNs at different mRNA doses. (F) Chemical structure of TE-EP8-S. (G) Secondary amine-rich cationic lipids outperformed their tertiary amine counterparts in mRNA delivery efficacy (0.25 mg/kg Fluc mRNA). (H) Quantification of mRNA expression in the spleen by secondary or tertiary amine-based CLNs. All data were presented as mean ± s.d., <i>n</i>=3.</p></div>
               </figcaption>
            </figure>
         </section>
         
         <p>To illustrate the differences between secondary and tertiary amine-based lipids in mRNA delivery, three groups of cationic lipids were selected to compare the protein expression in the spleen. Upon studying the SARs, it became evident that secondary amine relevant CLNs mediated significantly higher mRNA translation compared to their tertiary amine counterparts, highlighting the superiority of secondary amine-rich cationic lipids as one-component mRNA vectors (Figure <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0003">3G</a> and <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0003">3H</a>). Next, apart from the one-component utility, we incorporated PEG-lipid (1,2-dimyristoyl-rac-glycero-3-methoxy(poly(ethylene glycol-2000)), DMG-PEG2000) to formulate the two-component nano-carriers, effectively enabling selective mRNA expression in the lung (Figure S17–S18). These results were consistent with previous studies demonstrating that PEG-lipid incorporation into polymer-lipid vectors facilitated the effective lung-targeting.<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0024" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0024_R_d91932846e679" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 5b - J. Am. Chem. Soc.">5b</a>, <a href="#anie202405444-bib-0050" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0050_R_d91932846e679" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 13 - Angew. Chem. Int. Ed.">13</a></sup></span> This organ tropism shift could potentially be attributed to the formation of protein coronas on particle surfaces.<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0019" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0019_R_d91932846e682" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 4c - Proc. Natl. Acad. Sci. USA">4c</a>, <a href="#anie202405444-bib-0021" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0021_R_d91932846e682" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 4e - Proc. Natl. Acad. Sci. USA">4e</a></sup></span> Consequently, the proposed ionizable cationic lipids not only facilitate spleen specific mRNA delivery solely, but also enable efficacious extrahepatic delivery via synergistically functioning with other helper lipids.</p>
         
         <p>T cell engineering has shown significant promise in advancing cancer and viral infection therapeutics.<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0051" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0051_R_d91932846e688" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 14 - Angew. Chem. Int. Ed.">14</a></sup></span> For instance, FDA approved Chimeric Antigen Receptor T (CAR−T) cell therapy has garnered increasing attention, however, current ex vivo CAR−T cell engineering is complicated and costly.<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0026" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0026_R_d91932846e691" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 5d - Angew. Chem. Int. Ed.">5d</a></sup></span> In vivo T cell engineering has become increasingly appealing, but it faces challenges in efficiently delivering mRNA to T cells. To verify the capacity of CLNs to transfect T cells, a Ai9 mouse model with the LoxP flanked stop cassettes was used, which could induce tdTomato expression in the presence of Cre-recombinase protein to delete the stop cassettes (Figure <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0004">4A</a>).<span><sup><a href="#anie202405444-bib-0031" class="bibLink tab-link" id="anie202405444-bib-0031_R_d91932846e697" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 6d - Nat. Mater.">6d</a></sup></span> Consequently, TE-EP8-S CLNs loaded with Cre mRNA effectively facilitated protein expression in the spleen, with nearly 6 % of all T cells transfected following IV administration (Figure <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0004">4B</a>, <a href="#anie202405444-fig-0004">4C</a>, and S19). Combined with negligible in vivo toxicity (Figure S20–S21), these CLNs hold significant potential for immunotherapy applications, particularly in the context of CAR-T therapeutics.
</p>
         
         <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
            <figure class="figure" id="anie202405444-fig-0004"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/3b660920-fa49-46ae-bd36-e2e0d4bd1161/anie202405444-fig-0004-m.jpg"><picture>
                     <source srcset="/cms/asset/3b660920-fa49-46ae-bd36-e2e0d4bd1161/anie202405444-fig-0004-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/8a37eddf-f2d6-4361-881d-5ff4eff56ec0/anie202405444-fig-0004-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/3b660920-fa49-46ae-bd36-e2e0d4bd1161/anie202405444-fig-0004-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                  <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">Figure 4<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=anie202405444-fig-0004&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fanie.202405444" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                  </div>
                  <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">
                     <p>TE-EP8-S CLNs mediated mRNA translation in splenic T cells. (A) The cell types were analyzed by the Cre-LoxP mouse model, which could express tdTomato protein post Cre-recombinase mRNA expression to delete the stop cassette. (B) Representative flow dot plots of Td-Tomato<sup>+</sup> cells in the splenic cell subpopulations measured by flow cytometry. (C) Transfection quantification of Td-Tomato<sup>+</sup> splenic cell subpopulations. Data were presented as mean ± s.d., <i>n</i>=3.</p></div>
               </figcaption>
            </figure>
         </section>
         
         <p>In summary, to simplify and innovate targeting of current delivery systems, the one-component ionizable cationic lipids were developed to specifically deliver mRNA to the spleen. Unlike tertiary amine-rich cationic lipids typically employed in LNPs, the secondary amines in cationic lipids dramatically contributed to the vector property of one-component utility. Consequently, the one-component lipid carriers with secondary amines outperformed their tertiary amine counterparts, attributed to enhanced mRNA binding and cellular uptake. The top-performing CLNs mediated ultra-high mRNA expression in the spleen and T cells, showcasing substantial potential in cancer immunotherapy and vaccine development. This straightforward transformation strategy of cationic lipids provides an alternative option for mRNA delivery besides LNPs, simultaneously addressing the challenge of delivering mRNA to extrahepatic tissues. The secondary amine based one-component vector methodology proposed here can be expanded to a broad range of cationic lipids and polymers, paving the way for further advancements in mRNA delivery technology and applications.</p>
         
         <div class="article-section__content">
